Let me look at the workspace structure and check the Rocq installation.

The task: prove strong normalization for CBV STLC with locally nameless. The syntax is a bit unusual: T_Abs has `(forall x, ~ In x L -> <{ x |-> T1 ; Gamma |-- $(open t1 (tm_fvar x)) \in T2 }>)`. So terms are locally closed via opening with free variables. There's no `lc` premise in typing rules for variables/in general. Hmm, T_Var just requires `Gamma x = Some T`. Note the terms may contain bvars at top-level? T_Abs opens t1 with fvar x and requires typing under x |-> T1. Since we can pick x not in L, and the resulting term must be typed, but wait — does has_type allow bvar at all? T_Var requires tm_fvar. T_Abs's conclusion is tm_abs T1 t1, and the premise types open t1 (tm_fvar x) — but t1 itself may contain bvars, which get opened at the body. Actually open t1 (tm_fvar x) replaces bvar 0 with fvar x, but deeper bvars (under nested abstractions) remain.

For strong normalization, we need to handle bvars. Hmm, actually the standard approach: define the logical relation on terms and prove: for any well-typed term (which is locally closed in the appropriate sense?) Hmm. But has_type doesn't enforce local closure directly. Let me think: is every well-typed term locally closed? T_Var gives fvar x, closed. T_Abs: conclusion tm_abs T1 t1, whose local closure requires lc_at (S 0) t1, i.e. lc_at 1 t1. From the premise we know open t1 (tm_fvar x) is typed and, by induction, locally closed (lc_at 0 (open t1 (fvar x))). Does that imply lc_at 1 t1? Hmm, needs a lemma about opening. But that might be provable if we define the semantics correctly. However, we don't need local closure for SN necessarily—we need to handle bvars in conservative extension.

Wait, but actually the key issue: terms with free bvars can get stuck but that's fine, they are still strongly normalizing trivially if they don't step. But can a term with a bvar step? step requires app of abs to a value; the body is opened. Bvars could be substituted? open t v replaces bvar 0 with v. If v itself... values are locally closed. Hmm.

Better approach: standard "hereditarily terminating" logical relation (Tait / Girard), extended to CBV:

- For a type T, define SN_T as a predicate on terms.
  - SN_Bool t := strongly_normalizing t ∧ (t = true ∨ t = false) — hmm, but for CBV with values, we usually define: for base type, t is SN and any value it reduces to is true/false? Simpler: define a "good" predicate on terms of type Bool: strongly_normalizing and whenever t -->* v with v a value, v is true or false? That may be hard. Alternative: define value-based logical relation:
    - LR(Bool) = { t | SN t and (t value → t = true or t = false) }... Hmm, but since CBV closed typed terms are either values or step, we need the right notion.

Classic Tait for CBV: Define V_T (values of type T) and C_T (computations, i.e., terms of type T):
- V_Bool = { v | v = true ∨ v = false }, V_{A→B} = { v = λx.t | ∀ u ∈ C_A, t[u] ∈ C_B }.
- C_T = { t | SN t ∧ ∀ t', t -->* t' → (value t' → V_T t' ... ) }? Hmm.

Actually for strong normalization we can define a simpler predicate: 
C_T(t) := strongly_normalizing t ∧ (for every value v with t -->* v, V_T v).

But proving the fundamental theorem requires closure under application: if t1 ∈ C_{A→B} and t2 ∈ C_A then t1 t2 ∈ C_B. The problem is if t1 reduces to a non-value... standard.

Alternative simpler approach for SN (strong normalization): define SN_T as "strongly normalizing AND all reducts that are stuck? ". Since we only need SN not preservation, maybe define the logical relation as: LR_T(t) := strongly_normalizing t AND ... hmm.

Standard "reducibility" for SN in STLC: R_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ (for T = A → B: ∀ u, R_A(u) → R_B(t u)). For base type, R_Bool(t) := SN(t). Then fundamental theorem: if Γ ⊢ t : T and γ ∈ R_Γ, then t[γ] ∈ R_T. The application case: need t1 t2 ∈ R_T when t1 ∈ R_{T1→T2}, t2 ∈ R_{T1}. But t1 ∈ R_{T1→T2} only says SN(t1) and∀u∈R_{T1}, R_{T2}(t1 u). We need R_{T2}(t1 t2), which requires SN(t1 t2) and the ∀u part. SN(t1 t2) follows from SN(t1) and SN(t2) via standard lemma: if SN(t1), SN(t2), then SN(t1 t2) (for call-by-value). And the ∀ part: for u ∈ R_{T1}, t1 u ∈ R_{T2} — but we need (t1 t2) u? No wait: R_{T2}(t1 t2) where T2 = A → B requires ∀u, R_B((t1 t2) u). Hmm, we need SN(t1 t2 u) for u ∈ R_A. That requires SN(t1 t2) and SN(u) imply SN((t1 t2) u). Yes, by the same lemma applied to the term (t1 t2) and u. And then for R_B eventually base case: just SN. So define:

SN_T(t) := strongly_normalizing t if T = Bool; SN_T(t) := strongly_normalizing t ∧ ∀ u, SN_{T1}(u) → SN_{T2}(t u) if T = T1 → T2.

This is the standard "reducibility" for SN (strongly normalizing) — indeed this is Tait's reducibility for SN without needing values! Wait, but this works for call-by-name beta. For call-by-value, application reduction t1 t2 --> t1' t2 requires t2 locally closed, and the value restriction for ST_App2 means when t1 is a value (abs) t2 can reduce. The key lemma for CBV: if SN(t1) and SN(t2), then SN(t1 t2)? Let's check: t1 t2 can step via ST_App1 if t1 steps (t2 locally closed needed); ST_App2 if t1 is a value; or ST_AppAbs if t1 = abs and t2 is a value. The problematic case: t1 t2 with t1 an abstraction value and t2 not a value. Then t1 t2 steps via ST_App2 (if t2 steps). Or ST_AppAbs if t2 value. So inducting on SN(t1), SN(t2): reducts: t1' t2 with SN(t1') and SN(t2) → IH; v1 t2' with SN(t2') → IH; open t v with value v: need SN(open t v). Here t is the body of the abstraction; we know SN(t1) = SN(abs T t). From SN(abs T t) we know SN of its reducts but not SN of t or open t v. Hmm. So we need a lemma: SN(abs T t) → SN(t)? For locally closed t (lc_at 1 t), perhaps SN(t) relates. Actually SN of open body requires a substitution lemma. Hmm, but with the "reducibility" definition including t1 ∈ R_{T1→T2}, we know ∀u ∈ R_{T1}, SN(t1 u); but for ST_AppAbs we need SN(open t v).

Standard approach: prove fundamental theorem by defining the relation on values first, or use a different formulation. Let's think about the classical "hereditarily strongly normalizing" proof for CBV.

Maybe better: Use a semantic approach via "SN for open terms" and substitution lemmas. There is a well-known trick: define

- `sn t := strongly_normalizing t`.
- Define `R T t`:
  - `R Bool t := sn t`
  - `R (A → B) t := sn t ∧ ∀ u, R A u → R B (t u)`

Then prove:
1. If R T t then sn t (by definition for arrow, and base).
2. If sn t and sn u then sn (t u)? Not in general for CBV because of ST_AppAbs: t = abs T body; sn(abs T body) is given, but sn(open body u) isn't derivable from sn(abs T body). Hmm. Actually is it? SN(abs T body) means all reducts of abs T body are SN — but abs with lc is a value, no reducts unless body has bvars free... wait tm_abs is not a redex; step only on app. So SN(abs T body) gives nothing about body! Indeed SN(abs T body) holds for any body including Ω. So for the arrow case, R (A→B) t with t = abs T body needs: ∀u∈R A, R B (abs T body · u) which needs sn(abs T body u) which needs sn(open body u) — not derivable from sn(abs T body).

So the definition must incorporate body behavior. Classic: define R on values V_T and computations C_T as I wrote. Let's do Tait's method for CBV properly.

Define mutually:
- V_T(v): predicate on values (or on terms, with value implied).
- C_T(t): computation predicate.

Standard: 
V_Bool(v) := v = true ∨ v = false.
V_{A→B}(v) := v is an abstraction λx.t with lc, and ∀ u, C_A(u) → C_B(t[u]) (open t u).
C_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ ∀ v, t -->* v → value v → V_T(v). 

Hmm wait, for Bool we'd want closed terms of type Bool to be SN and reduce to true/false; but do we need the "∀ v, t -->* v → V_T v" part for base case? For proving SN only, C_Bool(t) := SN(t) suffices. But the app case of the fundamental theorem: we need C_{T2}(t1 t2) from C_{T1→T2}(t1) and C_{T1}(t2). If T2 = Bool, C_Bool = SN, so we need SN(t1 t2). From C_{T1→T2}(t1) we know SN(t1) and ∀v, t1 -->* v → value v → V_{T1→T2}(v). If t1 -->* v with v a value, then v is an abstraction λx.body and V says ∀u ∈ C_{T1}, C_{T2}(body[u]). And C_{T2} ⊆ SN. So to show SN(t1 t2) we use a general lemma: SN(t1) ∧ SN(t2) ∧ (∀ v, t1 -->* v → value v → ∀ ... hmm need C_{T2}(v t2)? Let me think.

Alternative cleaner: define C_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ ∀ v, t -->* v → value v → V_T(v), where V_T are value predicates that themselves imply C? Let's define:

V_Bool(v) := v = tm_true ∨ v = tm_false  (for any term v)
V_Arrow(A,B)(v) := exists T body, v = tm_abs T body ∧ lc_at 1 body ∧ ∀ u, C_A(u) → C_B(open body u)

C_T(t) := strongly_normalizing t ∧ ∀ v, (t -->* v) → value v → V_T v.

Then fundamental theorem: Γ ⊢ t : T, substitution σ mapping Γ's variables to closed terms with C_{Γ(x)} → C_T(t[σ]).

To prove fundamental theorem we need several lemmas:

(L1) C_T ⊆ SN by definition.
(L2) value v and V_T(v) → C_T(v): need SN(v) and any reduct of v that is a value is V_T. But values don't step at all (abs value with lc; true/false). SN(v) is immediate. And if v -->* v' with v' value, then v' = v (since step from a value is impossible). Need lemma: value v → ¬ (v --> t). Check: values are tm_abs T t with lc, tm_true, tm_false. step cases: app... Steps always produce app or open t v. Hmm, is `open t v` a value sometimes? For ST_AppAbs: app (abs T t) v --> open t v. And value of open t v? Could be, e.g., open (bvar 0) v = v. So the reduct of a redex can be a value. But a value itself never steps. Right.

(L3) Closure of C under "anti-reduction"/expansion: if t --> t' and SN(t') and ... hmm. We need: C_T(t') ∧ t --> t' → ... Actually to prove C_T(t) from reductions, use: SN(t) and all one-step reducts satisfy "∀ v, t' -->* v → value v → V_T v". Then for t -->* v with value v: if zero steps then t = v so C_T(t) means SN(v) and we need V_T(v) — hmm, in this case t itself is a value, need V_T(t). If at least one step: t --> t'' -->* v, and t'' satisfies the closure property giving V_T(v).

So structure: C_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ ∀ t', t -->* t' → value t' → V_T(t')? Wait if t is a value, then t -->* t (refl), so it includes V_T(t). Fine.

Fundamental theorem for application: t1 : A→B, t2 : A. IH: C_{A→B}(t1), C_A(t2). Need C_B(t1 t2).

Need: SN(t1 t2) from SN(t1), SN(t2), and the property that all value reducts of t1 are in V_{A→B}. Then need: all value reducts of t1 t2 lie in V_B.

Let me formulate key lemma (the "application/SN" lemma):

Lemma sn_app : ∀ t1 t2, SN(t1) → SN(t2) → (∀ v, t1 -->* v → value v → V_{A→B}(v)) → V_{A→B} is such that ... hmm, V depends on A,B. Let me phrase generically:

Actually the standard result: to prove C_B(t1 t2), we need
(a) SN(t1 t2)
(b) any value reduct v of t1 t2 satisfies V_B(v).

For (a): prove SN(t1 t2) by induction on SN(t1) and SN(t2) with an auxiliary condition: for every value v with t1 -->* v, and every value u with t2 -->* u, SN(open body u) where v = abs T body... Hmm, the statement "∀ value v reachable from t1, V_{A→B}(v)" gives for v = abs T body: ∀ u ∈ C_A, C_B(open body u), in particular for u a value with V_A(u) → C_B(open body u) → SN(open body u). But C_A(u) requires SN(u) and all value reducts V_A. Hmm, we know value u and V_A(u) → C_A(u) by L2. So SN(open body u) follows.

But in the SN(t1 t2) induction, the ST_AppAbs case gives open body u where u is a value reached from t2 (since t1 t2 -->* v u with v value, u value?). Let's structure:

Lemma sn_app_general: For terms t1 t2, if SN(t1), SN(t2), and ∀ v, t1 -->* v → value v → V_{A→B}(v), then SN(t1 t2), and moreover ∀ v, t1 t2 -->* v → value v → V_B(v)? Hmm, the second part: if t1 t2 -->* v with v a value, then... this is a "value reduct" property, which we could perhaps prove separately.

Hmm, this is getting complex. Let's think of an alternative formulation that's simpler to mechanize: use the "strongly normalizing" inductive predicate and define the logical relation as a "hereditarily SN" predicate directly on terms with values built in. There's a classical trick for CBV SN by "reducibility candidates" where you define:

R_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ ∀ v, t -->* v → value v → V_T(v)

with V as above. Then fundamental theorem. Alternatively, define R on values only, and define the "computation" relation as SN plus value reducts.

I think this standard approach is fine but the proofs need care. Let me think about what's needed precisely, and find the simplest mechanizable route.

Alternatively, maybe simpler: use a different well-founded measure approach? For STLC without fixpoints, we can define a measure: a term's "size" decreases under reduction? For β-reduction in CBV, the size of (λx.t) v is 1 + |t| + |v|, and open t v size ≤ |t| + |v| (substitution can duplicate v: |t[v/x]| can be up to |t| * |v|, actually substitution duplicates). So multiset ordering or the standard "SN by logical relation" is needed. We could also use a translation to de Bruijn and use existing library? No.

Alternative approach: Use "hereditarily strongly normalizing" via a recursive definition on types with well-founded recursion? The definition R_Arrow refers to R_A and R_B positively, so it's a positive inductive definition; we could define it as an Inductive for each type? In Rocq, define by recursion over ty returning a Prop? Since ty is an inductive type, we can define a Fixpoint `lr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop` using a Fixpoint on T, since recursive occurrences are on smaller types (T1 and T2 are subterms). Then define LR_Arrow as `fun t => strongly_normalizing t /\ forall u, lr T1 u -> lr T2 (tm_app t u)` but the "forall u" in a Fixpoint definition is fine (positive occurrence under arrows but lr T1, lr T2 are recursive calls on smaller structures). Yes: 

Fixpoint lr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => strongly_normalizing t /\ forall u, lr T1 u -> lr T2 (tm_app t u)
  end.

This is a valid Fixpoint (structurally recursive on T). This is the standard "Tait reducibility without values" for call-by-name. Question: does it work for CBV? The issue: ST_AppAbs requires SN(open t v) where abs T t is a value. From lr (A→B) (abs T t) we get SN(abs T t) (nothing useful) and ∀u, lr A u → lr B (abs T t · u). Taking u with lr A u (there might be no closed u of type A! But actually there is — for each type A there exists a closed term of type A: e.g., Bool: true; A→B: lambda. So we can find u, but we need to derive SN(open t u) from lr B (abs T t · u). lr B (app (abs T t) u) = SN(app (abs T t) u) ∧ ... The SN of app gives: all reducts SN, including open t u (since t closed? ST_AppAbs requires lc_at 1 t, i.e. locally_closed (abs T t) which holds if value (abs T t) holds — value requires locally_closed). So if u is a value, app (abs T t) u --> open t u, so SN(open t u) follows from SN(app (abs T t) u). Good. But we need SN(open t v) for arbitrary value v of type A, and we know lr A v only if the fundamental theorem gives it. Hmm.

So the fundamental theorem with this lr (the call-by-name style) would go: Γ ⊢ t : T, for all σ with ∀x ∈ Γ, lr_{Γ(x)}(σ x), we have lr_T(t[σ]). The application case: need lr_B(t1 t2) where lr_{A→B}(t1) means SN(t1) and ∀u, lr_A u → lr_B(t1 u). We need lr_B(t1 t2), i.e., SN(t1 t2) and ∀u, lr_A u → lr_B(t1 t2 u). The second: for u with lr_A u, need lr_B((t1 t2) u). Since lr_B(t1 t2) would give ∀u, lr_A u → lr_B(t1 t2 u). So it suffices to prove SN(t1 t2) and the "∀u" part. Hmm, circular-ish: to show lr_B(t1 t2) it suffices to show SN(t1 t2) ∧ ∀u, lr_A u → lr_B(t1 t2 u). And lr_B(t1 t2 u) = SN(t1 t2 u) ∧ ∀w,... — inductively t1 t2 u tacks more arguments. Since types are finite, this recursion terminates: at each step the type decreases. So define the property:

P_A(t) := lr_A(t). lr_{A→B}(t) = SN(t) ∧ ∀u, lr_A u → lr_B(t u).

To show lr_B(t1 t2): need SN(t1 t2) plus ∀u, lr_A u → lr_B(t1 t2 u). Both could follow from a generalized lemma: if lr_{A1→...→An→C}(t) and lr_{A1}(u1), ..., then ... SN(t u1 ... un) and for value reducts etc.

The critical lemma needed: SN(t1) → SN(t2) → SN(t1 t2), for locally closed terms? Let's check whether that's true for CBV with the caveat about ST_AppAbs. Take t1 = abs T body with SN(abs T body) trivially true (it's a value, stuck) but body = Ω which loops when opened/applied to something? Then t1 t2 with t2 = some value u: steps to open body u which could loop. But wait — is t1 = abs T body locally closed required for ST_AppAbs and ST_App2? ST_AppAbs requires locally_closed (abs T body), ST_App2 requires value v1 which requires locally_closed (abs T body). If body is not locally closed (contains bvar >= 1 free), then abs T body is not a value, and it's stuck (can't step). Then t1 t2 with t1 not a value: ST_App1 requires t1 --> t1' — t1 stuck, so t1 t2 is stuck too. So it's SN. But the ST_AppAbs case can't fire because t1 isn't a value. So the lemma SN(t1) → SN(t2) → SN(t1 t2) requires t1 locally closed? Let's see: if t1 = abs T body is locally closed and SN (trivially), body can still be Ω-like: body = app (abs ...) ... locally closed, e.g. body = (λx. x x) (λx. x x) — locally closed. Then t1 = abs T body is a value (locally closed) and t1 t2 steps to open body t2 which loops. But SN(t1) holds trivially. So lemma "SN(t1) ∧ SN(t2) → SN(t1 t2)" is FALSE for CBV when t1 is an abstraction value. So the naive lr with just SN is insufficient. This is exactly why we need value-based logical relations where V_Arrow(v) requires body behavior.

OK so let's do the proper thing. Let me define:

Inductive value already given (with locally_closed requirements). Define:

Fixpoint lr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      forall v, t -->* v -> value v ->
        (exists Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body /\ lc_at 1 body /\
           forall u, lr T1 u -> lr T2 (open body u))
  end.

Hmm, but this mixes: the "∀v reachable value" is essentially the C predicate. Maybe define:

Definition sn_vals (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  strongly_normalizing t /\ (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> lr T v)  -- for arrow
and for Bool, require forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> v = true \/ v = false?

Actually here's a thought: do we need the base case to say anything about reachable values? For SN purposes, C_Bool(t) = SN(t) suffices. But for the arrow case we need V_T for values. And for V_Arrow, we need to know the value's body. So:

lr_T(t) for computations:
- lr_Bool(t) := strongly_normalizing t
- lr_Arrow(A,B)(t) := strongly_normalizing t ∧ ∀ v, t -->* v → value v → V_{A→B}(v)

where V_{A→B}(v) := ∃ Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body ∧ lc_at 1 body ∧ ∀ u, lr_A(u) → lr_B(open body u).

Hmm, the lc_at 1 body is automatic from value v = tm_abs Tbody body (value gives locally_closed which unfolds to lc_at 1 body). Fine.

But wait: for lr_Arrow, we don't need the parameter Tbody to match... v is an abstraction, type doesn't matter for semantics. OK.

Alternatively define:

Fixpoint lr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      forall v, t -->* v -> value v ->
        exists body, v = tm_abs T1 body /\ forall u, lr T1 u -> lr T2 (open body u)
  end.

Hmm, the type annotation on the binder: the value reached is tm_abs Tbody body for some Tbody; requiring Tbody = T1 is not derivable (typing gives that abs's annotation... in CBV, the annotation is irrelevant to evaluation; the typed term's abs has annotation T1 presumably from T_Abs inversion: t1 = tm_abs T1' t1' with T1' possibly anything? T_Abs rule: conclusion has lambda : T1, body where the type is exactly T1. So inversion on has_type (tm_abs T_annot body) : T gives T = T1' -> T2' and T_annot = T1'? Wait the rule: `T_Abs : forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1, ... -> <{ Gamma |-- lambda : T1, t1 \in T1 -> T2 }>`. The conclusion's annotation is T1 and type is T1 -> T2, so annotation matches left side of arrow. So inversion gives Tbody = T1 indeed. But for the semantic relation we don't need that, so let me not require it, to be safe. Instead: V_Arrow(v) := ∃ Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body ∧ ∀ u, lr T1 u → lr T2 (open body u).

But then lr (T1→T2) t = SN(t) ∧ ∀ v, t -->* v → value v → ∃ Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body ∧ ∀ u, lr T1 u → lr T2 (open body u).

Hmm, this requires proving for the fundamental theorem that when t = abs T1 body (the abstraction from T_Abs), the arrow relation holds: SN(abs T1 body) — true since value, but need locally_closed for value: does has_type give locally_closed (abs T1 body)? We need to prove lc_at 1 body from typing! Hmm. T_Abs premise: ∀x ∉ L, has_type (x|->T1;Γ) (open body (fvar x)) T2. We need to derive lc_at 1 body. Strategy: prove a general lemma: has_type Γ t T → locally_closed? No wait, if Γ has entries, fvars are allowed. Locally closed means no free bvars: lc_at 0 t. Define "all bvars are < k". For a term typed under Γ, all its bvars should be < 0? Hmm, free variables (fvar) are fine in lc_at (lc_fvar allows any). So has_type Γ t T → lc_at 0 t? Let's check T_Var: fvar x is lc_at 0. T_App: components. T_Abs: need lc_at 1 body from typing of open body (fvar x). So we need a lemma: lc_at 0 (open body (fvar x)) → lc_at 1 body. That's plausible: opening bvar 0 with a locally closed (the fvar is lc_at 0... actually lc_at k (fvar x) for any k) term. Lemma: if lc_at k u (for all k? fvar is lc_at any k) and lc_at k (open_rec m u body) with m < k... then lc_at k body? Let's think: open_rec m u body replaces bvar m with u. If lc_at k (open_rec m u body) and lc_at k u (all k), and m < k? then lc_at k body. Hmm, because if body has bvar i, and i = m, then the result has u at that position, lc_at k u ok. If i ≠ m, then bvar i is checked with i < k. But careful: bvar m in body that gets replaced: fine. But if body has bvar i > m under a nested binder? open_rec increments k? No: open_rec k u t recurses with S k under abs; the "k" in open_rec is the depth. open_rec m u body replaces bvar whose index equals m? Let's look:

Fixpoint open_rec (k : nat) (u : tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => if Nat.eqb k i then u else tm_bvar i
  | ...
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) u t1)
  ...

Now, main structure. Let's think again about what the cleanest mechanizable logical relation is.

Option A (value-based, C predicate with reachable values):

Define:
```
Fixpoint lr (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> value_lr T1 T2 v
  end
with value_lr ...
```
Can't easily have two mutually recursive fixpoints where value_lr calls lr — it can: mutual Fixpoint with structurally decreasing T in both. Let's define:

Fixpoint lr (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> vlr T1 T2 v)
  end
with vlr (T1 T2 : ty) (v : tm) {struct T1} : Prop :=
  exists Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body /\ (forall u, lr T1 u -> lr T2 (open body u)).

Hmm, but Coq's mutual fixpoint requires the recursive calls to be on structurally smaller arguments. In `lr (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)`, calls `vlr T1 T2 v` with T1 a subterm of the matched T — is that accepted? The Fixpoint is on T; in the branch T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2, T1 is a strict subterm. `vlr T1 T2` — the first argument T1 decreases. But vlr's principal argument is T1 (struct T1), and T2 is a parameter-ish (but it's not uniform since it varies). Coq might accept a mutual fixpoint. However, in vlr, the call `lr T1 u` and `lr T2 (open body u)` — T2 is a subterm of T1? No! In vlr T1 T2, T2 is not necessarily a subterm of T1 (it's the second component). Hmm, when called from lr (Ty_Arrow T1 T2), the arguments are T1 and T2, both subterms of the original T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2, so T2 is structurally smaller than T. But within vlr's body, T2 is just a parameter; the recursive call lr T2 would need T2 < T1 which isn't true in general.

Could restructure: vlr takes the whole arrow type T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2 as principal argument, with T1, T2 obtained by matching:

Fixpoint lr (T : ty) (t : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, t -->* v -> value v ->
         exists Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body /\
           (forall u, lr T1 u -> lr T2 (open body u)))
  end.

This is a single Fixpoint, calls lr T1 and lr T2 with T1, T2 subterms of T. Fine! No need for mutual fixpoint. Great, this is simple.

But is this relation adequate? Let's check the application case of the fundamental theorem: lr_{A→B}(t1) gives SN(t1) and "reachable values are abs with good body". lr_A(t2). Need lr_B(t1 t2).

Case B = Bool: need SN(t1 t2). Prove lemma: SN(t1) → (∀ v, t1 -->* v → value v → good_abs_A→B v) → lr_A(t2) → SN(t1 t2)?? Hmm, we need the body behavior with argument t2, but t2 isn't a value necessarily. ST_AppAbs requires the argument be a value: t1 t2 with t1 = abs, t2 not value: steps only via ST_App2 (t2 --> t2'). So the step open body u only occurs when t2 -->* u and u is a value. But t2 ∈ lr_A doesn't give that t2 reduces to a value (for A arrow, a term like a stuck bvar? but t2 is closed typed so it does reduce to a value; but semantically lr doesn't say so).

Hmm. So proving SN(t1 t2) from SN(t1), SN(t2) requires: for all t2' with t2 -->* t2', and all v1 with t1 -->* v1 value, corresponding to a state where t1 t2 -->* v1 t2', and if t2' is a value then t1 t2 -->* v1 t2' --> open body t2' (if v1 = abs and lc holds). So need SN(open body t2') for t2' a value reachable from t2. We know good_abs says ∀u, lr_A u → lr_B(open body u). If we know lr_A(t2') for reachable values t2' of t2? We know t2 ∈ lr_A. For A = Bool, lr_A(t2') = SN(t2') — we have that. For A = Arrow, lr_A(t2) gives reachable values V_A. Is lr_A(t2') for t2' a value implied? lr_A(t2') = SN(t2') ∧ reachable values good. We know t2 ∈ lr_A gives: for all v reachable from t2, value v → good_abs v. So yes, t2' reachable from t2 (multi transitive) and value → good_abs. And vlr_A(t2') where vlr is "∃ body, v = abs ... ∧ ∀u, lr_A1 u → lr_A2(open body u)". Good.

So the key: from t ∈ lr_T and t -->* t', value t' derive lr_T(t'). This is a closure lemma ("lr is closed under multistep to a value"). For T = Bool: lr_Bool(t') = SN(t'), and SN(t) → t -->* t' → SN(t') ✓. For arrow: SN(t') and reachable values good — reachable from t' implies reachable from t (transitivity) ✓ plus SN. Let me think about an approach known to work: For CBV, one can use the logical relation:

Definition R_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ (∀ v, t -->* v → value v → V_T(v))
V_Bool(v) := v = true ∨ v = false
V_{A→B}(v) := ∃ body, v = abs _ body ∧ ∀ u, R_A(u) → R_B(open body u)

This is the standard, and the proofs are:

1. R_T(t) → SN(t).
2. For value v: V_T(v) → R_T(v). (requires SN(v) since values don't step, and reachable values = v itself with multi_refl).
   Wait, R_T(v) for T = B: SN(v) ✓. For T=arrow: SN(v) and reachable values: v -->* v' with v' value → v' = v by no-step-from-value + multi inversion. Need V_T(v) ✓.
3. Closure: R_T(t), t -->* t', value t' → R_T(t'). (transitivity of multi + SN closure).
4. Application: R_{A→B}(t1) → R_A(t2) → R_B(t1 t2).

Proving (4): Let's denote t1 t2. Need SN(t1 t2) and reachable-value goodness.

So then we need a lemma:

Let me look at this from the perspective of known formalizations. In Software Foundations' "StlcStrongNorm" (Norm.v), the proof uses a logical relation for call-by-name:

Definition R (T:ty) (t:tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Bool => SN t
  | T1 --> T2 => SN t ∧ forall t', R T1 t' -> R T2 (t t')
  end

and the fundamental theorem is by induction on typing; the T_Abs case requires a lemma:

Lemma R_abs : forall T1 T2 t1, (forall t2, R T1 t2 -> R T2 (open t1 t2)) -> R (T1-->T2) (abs T1 t1).

Then SN(app (abs t1) t2) needs SN(open t1 t2) which follows from the hypothesis (given SN(t2) and ... hmm, the hypothesis gives R_{T2}(open t1 t2), which gives SN). And reductions inside t2 with abs at head: ST_App2. So the same structure. And for arrow T2, R_{T1'→T2'}(app (abs t1) t2) requires ∀t2', R_{T1'}(t2') → R_{T2'}(app (abs t1) t2 t2').   end.
```

Lemma R_app_R : ∀ A B t u, R_{A→B}(t)?? ... circular.

Let me step back and design a cleaner logical relation known to work. There's a classic neat approach for CBV SN: use "reducibility candidates" defined by:

R_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ ∀v, t -->* v → value v → V_T(v)
V_Bool(v) := True  (or v=true ∨ v=false)
V_{A→B}(v) := ∀u, R_A(u) → R_B(open (body v) u)

where for a value v that is not an abstraction, V is True/False accordingly. Since only abstractions matter, define V_{A→B}(v) := ∀ (Tbody body), v = abs Tbody body → ∀u, R_A(u) → R_B(open body u). This handles non-abs values vacuously. Then:

Key lemmas:
1. R_T(t) → SN(t).
2. R_T(t) closed under step (and multistep): t ∈ R_T, t --> t' → t' ∈ R_T.
3. v value, V_T(v) → R_T(v).
4. If t ∈ R_{A→B}, u ∈ R_A then t u ∈ R_B.

For 4: We prove by induction on B? Let's try more carefully with the V definition above.

Goal R_B(t u) where t ∈ R_{A→B}, u ∈ R_A.
- B = Bool: R_Bool(t u) = SN(t u) ∧ ∀ v, t u -->* v → value v → True. So just SN(t u).
- B = B1→B2: SN(t u) ∧ ∀v, t u -->* v → value v → V_{B1→B2}(v).

So both need SN(t u), and the arrow case needs value-reduct analysis.

Proving SN(t u): Let's try induction on SN-t and SN-u with hypothesis H: ∀v, t -->* v → value v → V_{A→B}(v) [i.e., effectively R_{A→B}(t)]. Actually t ∈ R_{A→B} gives SN(t) and H.

Claim: SN(t u) given SN(t), H, R_A(u).
Induction on SN(t) (the derivation SN_intro t Ht where Ht : ∀t', t --> t' → SN t'), then nested induction on SN(u)? In Rocq, we can do `induction` on the SN derivation (SN_intro) giving IH over all one-step reducts; but we also need to recurse on u. So state the lemma as: ∀ t, SN(t) → ∀ u, R_A(u) → H(t) → SN(t u), and prove by induction on SN(t); inside, for each step app t u --> t', need SN(t'), which requires case analysis; in ST_App2 case (t = v1 value, u --> u'), we need SN(v1 u') which is not directly IH (u' is a reduct of u, and our IH is on SN-prefix of t). Hmm, need simultaneous induction: we can do induction on SN(t), then in ST_App2 case, use an auxiliary lemma which is an induction on SN(u) with the value v1 fixed.

Standard technique: prove by induction on SN(t) a statement that also universally quantifies u with R_A(u):
Lemma SN_app_l : ∀ t, SN t → (∀ v, t -->* v → value v → V_{A→B}(v)) → ∀ u, R_A u → SN (t u).
Proof: intros t HSN. induction HSN as [t Ht IH]. intros H u HR.
  apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. inversion Hstep.
  - ST_AppAbs: t = abs T body, u value, t' = open body u. From H (with multi_refl, value(abs)) get V_{A→B}(abs T body): ∀u, R_A(u) → R_B(open body u). Then R_B → SN ✓.
  - ST_App1: t --> t1', t' = app t1' u. Need SN(app t1' u): apply IH to t1' (Ht t1' Hstep... careful naming) with hypothesis H' (∀v, t1' -->* v → value v → V(v)) derived from H via multi transitivity, and HR. ✓.
  - ST_App2: t is value v1, u --> u', t' = app v1 u'. Need SN(v1 u'). Hmm, here t = v1 is a value. We can't apply IH on t (no reduct). We need a different argument: induction on SN(u) with v1 fixed. So the proof must handle this case using a lemma:

Lemma SN_app_r : ∀ v1, value v1 → (∀ u, R_A u → ... hmm.

Let me restructure: prove SN(t u) by induction on SN(u) as well. The natural statement:

Lemma SN_app : ∀ t u, SN t → SN u → (∀ v, t -->* v → value v → V_{A→B}(v)) → R_A(u) → SN(t u).

Hmm, but the ST_App1 case needs IH on t with the same u, and the ST_App2 case needs IH on u with the same t = v1. This is a classic "double induction" which can be done by induction on SN(t) with a nested induction on SN(u) — but the nesting order matters: In ST_App1 we need the result for (t', u) with t' a reduct of t, same u; a nested induction on SN(u) inside would work fine; in ST_App2 we need (v1, u') with u' a reduct of u; if the outer induction is on SN(t) and t = v1 doesn't change, then the inner induction on SN(u) can be applied. So prove:

Lemma SN_app : ∀ t, SN t → ∀ u, SN u → (∀v, t -->* v → value v → V_{A→B}(v)) → R_A(u) → SN(t u).
Proof: induction on SN(t) derivation. intro u, then induction on SN(u) derivation (or actually we can do both inductions via `induction HSN1; induction HSN2`? Careful: the hypotheses H and R_A must be generalized appropriately).

Actually in Rocq, `induction` on the SN derivation with the right generalization is tricky because the IH needs to be stated with all extra hypotheses universally quantified. Standard: 
```
Lemma SN_app : forall t, SN t -> forall u, SN u -> (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V v) -> R_A u -> SN (app t u).
Proof.
  intros t Ht. induction Ht as [t Ht IH].
  intros u Hu. induction Hu as [u Hu IHu].
  ...
```
Wait: induction Hu after introducing t and Ht. The IH (from Ht) is: ∀t', t --> t' → ∀u, SN u → (...) → R_A u → SN(app t' u) — this IH is in context with the generalized u? The induction on Ht: `induction Ht` will produce IH : forall t' : tm, t --> t' -> SN (t' u)?? No — because u was not introduced before the induction, the IHs will be generalized over the remaining goals... Hmm. In Rocq, if the goal is `forall u, ...` when you do induction on Ht, the IH is `forall t', t --> t' -> forall u, SN u -> ... -> SN (app t' u)`, provided u is still universally quantified in the goal at induction time. Yes! So:

```
intros t Ht.
induction Ht as [t Ht IH].   (* goal: forall u, SN u -> H -> R_A u -> SN (app t u) *)
intros u Hu.
induction Hu as [u Hu IHu].  (* goal: H -> R_A u -> SN(app t u); IHs: for u', u --> u' -> H -> R_A u' -> SN(app t u'), plus IH from Ht: for t', t-->t' -> forall u, SN u -> H_t' -> R_A u -> SN(app t' u) *)
```
Hmm, careful: after induction Hu, the IH from Ht is in context and mentions `u`? The IH from Ht was established before u was introduced, so it is ∀t', t --> t' → ∀u, SN u → (∀v, t' -->* v → value v → V v) → R_A u → SN(t' u). Good. The IH from Hu: ∀u', u --> u' → (∀v, t -->* v → value v → V v) → R_A u' → SN(app t u'). Good.

Now main goal: SN(app t u). Apply SN_intro; intros t' Hstep; inversion Hstep.
- ST_AppAbs: t = abs T body, u value, t' = open body u. H: ∀v, t -->* v → value v → V(v). t = abs, so V(abs T body) = ∀u, R_A u → R_B(open body u) (with V defined as: ∀ Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body → ∀u, R_A u → R_B(open body u)). Then R_B(open body u) → SN ✓ (need lemma R_B → SN).
- ST_App1: t --> t1', t' = app t1' u. Apply IH from Ht with u, SN u (Hu gives SN u), H_t' (from H and multistep transitivity: if t' -->* v then t -->* v), R_A u ✓.
- ST_App2: value t (v1), u --> u', t' = app t u'. Apply IHu with H (same t) and R_A u' (from R_A u closure under step: R_A(u) and u --> u' gives R_A(u')). ✓.

Great, this works! Note: in ST_App1, the IH from Ht requires SN u — we have Hu : SN u ✓. And requires H_t' which we derive.

But subtlety: The step constructor ST_App1 requires `locally_closed t2` — irrelevant for us. ST_AppAbs requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T t)` and `value v`; our H gives V(abs T body) using value(abs) as hypothesis; V requires the shape which is given by inversion of the step: t = abs T body, u value. Hmm, H requires `t -->* v` with v value; here v = t = abs T body, multi_refl, and value (abs T body) which comes from the ST_AppAbs constructor's premise ✓ (actually the ST_AppAbs premise has locally_closed (tm_abs T t) and value v; value(abs) is not directly given but locally_closed(abs) means we can build value(abs) via v_abs ✓).

Good. So SN(t u) is provable given R_A(u) and H (the "V-reduct" property of t). Note: R_A(u) → SN u (lemma), so actually SN u comes free; but we used SN u in the induction, and Hu is available from R_A u (we can get it). But we also need SN u for the nested induction — we can get SN u from R_A u via lemma R_SN. Fine, or keep both.

OK let me now carefully write the whole proof plan in the body-based formulation and check each obligation.

Definitions:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T1 T2 v)
  end
with V (T1 T2 : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  forall Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body ->
     forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open body u).
```
Mutual fixpoint issue again: V's body calls R T1 and R T2 where T2 is a parameter, not structurally smaller than T1. Problem. Let's avoid mutual fixpoint by making V a Fixpoint over the arrow type T itself:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
        forall Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body ->
          forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open body u))
  end.
```
This is a single Fixpoint on T, with recursive calls on T1 and T2, both strict subterms. ✓ Rocq accepts.

So define:
R_Bool(t) := SN(t).
R_{A→B}(t) := SN(t) ∧ ∀v, t -->* v → value v → ∀Tbody body, v = abs Tbody body → ∀u, R_A(u) → R_B(open body u).

This is a "value-reduct" style relation where the value clause includes the body behavior directly (absorbing V). This avoids mutual recursion. The "V" notion can be defined as a derived property:

Definition V (T1 T2 : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  ∀ Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body → ∀ u, R T1 u → R T2 (open body u).

Then R_{A→B}(t) = SN(t) ∧ ∀v, t -->* v → value v → V A B v. Hmm, we can't write it that way in the Fixpoint because V is defined after R. But we can define V after R as a Definition and prove the equivalence: R_{A→B}(t) ↔ SN(t) ∧ ∀v, t -->* v → value v → V A B v. Actually since R's arrow branch is literally the same formula, we can just prove a lemma `R_arrow_iff : R (Ty_Arrow A B) t <-> strongly_normalizing t /\ forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V A B v.` by unfolding.

Good. Now let's list lemmas:

L1. R_SN : ∀ T t, R T t → SN t.
   Proof: induction on T; simpl; intros; [exact H | destruct H; exact H].
   Careful: R is a Fixpoint; destruct on T, simpl in H. For Bool: H : SN t. For arrow: H : SN t ∧ ... → exact (proj1 H).

L2. Value SN and closure of multi:
   - value_no_step : ∀ v t, value v → ¬ (v --> t).
     Proof: destruct on value v: v_abs → inversion on step: step cases all have app or ... hmm, step constructor conclusions: ST_AppAbs: tm_app ... --> ...; ST_App1: tm_app; ST_App2: tm_app. So inversion on step with source tm_abs T t: no case matches (app ≠ abs). Actually `inversion H` where H : tm_abs T t --> t' should solve it as the constructors' conclusions are apps. Similarly true/false. So: intros v t Hv Hstep; destruct Hv; inversion Hstep. Might need dependent destruction? The step constructors conclude `tm_app ... --> ...` and our hypothesis is `tm_abs T t --> t'`. Inversion should work because the indices don't unify (tm_abs vs tm_app). ✓
   - value_SN : ∀ v, value v → SN v. Proof: SN_intro; intros t' Ht'; exfalso; apply value_no_step in Ht'; exact Ht'. Need: value v → value v → ¬ step. 
   - multi_trans : ∀ x y z, x -->* y → y -->* z → x -->* z. Induction on first.

   - multi_value: value v → v -->* t' → t' = v. Proof: induction on multistep: refl → t' = v; step: value v and v --> y contradicts value_no_step.

L3. SN of open body u given R_B(open body u): immediate from R_SN.

L4. Core: R_abs_intro / beta lemma. Let me define the main lemmas carefully.

Let's define the key lemma set. I'll try to prove in this order:

(a) R_SN : R T t → SN t.

(b) R_step_closed / R_multi_closed: R T t → t -->* t' → R T t'? Wait, is R closed under multistep? R_T(t) = SN(t) ∧ value-reduct clause. SN(t) → SN(t') (multi_SN) ✓. Value-reduct clause: t' -->* v → t -->* v by multi trans ✓. So yes, R_multi_closed: R T t → t -->* t' → R T t'.

Actually careful for Bool: R_Bool(t) = SN(t), so SN(t) → t -->* t' → SN(t') ✓ same lemma.

(c) R_value: value v → R T v? For T = Bool: SN(v) ✓. For arrow: SN(v) ✓ and value-reduct clause: v -->* v' with v' value → v' = v; then need ∀Tbody body, v = abs Tbody body → ∀u, R_A u → R_B(open body u). But that's exactly the V property of v which we don't know automatically! For a value v that is an abstraction, R_{A→B}(v) requires its body to satisfy the relation. So "value → R_T" is FALSE for arrow types without extra hypotheses. Right, that's the point: R is the reducibility predicate.

So we need: for v = abs Tbody body with the body behavior assumption, R_{A→B}(v). That's the abs-intro lemma:
   R_abs : ∀ A B Tbody body, lc_at 1 body? → (∀u, R A u → R B (open body u)) → R (A→B) (abs Tbody body).
   Proof: SN(abs) ✓ (need no_step: SN_intro; intros; inversion on value?). Value-reduct clause: v = abs; v -->* w with value w. Since abs is a value (need locally_closed (abs Tbody body) → value), value_no_step and multi_value give w = abs Tbody body. Then w = abs Tbody body and the hypothesis applies. So we need value(abs) which needs locally_closed (abs Tbody body), i.e., lc_at 1 body. So hypothesis lc_at 1 body.

Hmm, so R_abs needs lc_at 1 body. In the fundamental theorem's T_Abs case, we need lc_at 1 body, which we get from typing (lc lemma). ✓

(d) The beta/app lemma (the hard one). Let's formulate:

Lemma R_app_aux : ∀ A B t u,
  strongly_normalizing t →
  (∀ v, t -->* v → value v → ∀ Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body → ∀ w, R A w → R B (open body w)) →
  R A u →
  R B (tm_app t u).

Note the second hypothesis is exactly the value-clause of R_{A→B}(t) (unfolded), and it's what we get from t ∈ R_{A→B} together with SN(t). So this lemma proves: if t ∈ R_{A→B} and u ∈ R_A then t u ∈ R_B. We'll package as R_app.

Proof strategy: as discussed, double induction on SN(t) and SN(u), and goal R_B(t u) = SN(t u) ∧ (if B arrow: ∀v, t u -->* v → value v → V_B(v)).

Let's try to prove it as one induction with the goal R_B(t u) directly. Since R_B(t u) for arrow includes the value clause, maybe we should induct on B as well? Let's see: if B = Bool, goal SN(t u). If B = B1→B2, goal SN(t u) ∧ value clause. The value clause requires V_{B1→B2}(v) = ∀Tbody body, v = abs Tbody body → ∀w, R_{B1} w → R_{B2}(open body w). This requires proving R_{B2}(open body w) for arbitrary w ∈ R_{B1} — quite hard from the reachable-value structure. Hmm, but as noted, we can get R_B(v) from the reduction structure, then the value clause of R_B(v) (with refl) gives V_B(v). So let's structure the proof of R_B(t u) as:

- SN(t u): proven by the double induction.
- Value clause (B arrow): for v with t u -->* v, value v: analyze. We can prove this by induction on the multistep derivation using the SN structure? Let's think differently: maybe prove the value clause simultaneously with SN in the double induction, using the following observation:

If t u -->* v with v a value, then either:
 (i) the first step is AppAbs: t = abs Tbody body, u value, and t u --> open body u -->* v. Then the hypothesis (value clause for t with value(abs) and multi_refl) gives ∀w∈R_A, R_B(open body w). With w = u: R_B(open body u). Then R_multi_closed gives R_B(v). Then since v is a value, R_B(v) with refl gives V_B(v) ✓.
 (ii) the first step is App1: t --> t1, t u --> t1 u -->* v. Use IH (on SN t) with t1: we need the value clause for t1, which follows from the hypothesis of t (multi trans). Then IH gives R_B(t1 u), whose value clause gives V_B(v) ✓. Also SN(t1 u) ✓.
 (iii) the first step is App2: t is a value, u --> u1, t u --> t u1 -->* v. Use IH on SN u (inner induction) with u1 and R_A(u1) (closure). Then R_B(t u1) gives value clause → V_B(v) ✓ and SN ✓.

So if we prove by double induction the statement R_B(t u) (including both components), the cases work! But the induction needs to handle the goal R_B(t u) where the value clause is part of it; in case (i) we need the hypothesis of t to give R_B(open body u) — we have it. In cases (ii),(iii), we need IH to give R_B(t1 u) or R_B(t u1) with the respective hypotheses — available if the induction statement includes the value clause and SN. Good.

But there's a subtlety in case (i): after the first step AppAbs, we have R_B(open body u), and then open body u -->* v with v value, so R_B(v) by multi closure, then V_B(v) by the value clause with refl. ✓. But wait: in case (i) we also need... the first step of t u -->* v: what if t u is already v? t u is an app, never a value, so if v is a value, there's at least one step. But the multistep derivation could be multi_refl only if t u = v, impossible since app ≠ value constructor. Need to handle: when analyzing "t u -->* v", we do induction on the multistep derivation. If multi_refl, then t u = v, but v is a value — contradiction. So need a lemma app_not_value : ¬ value (tm_app t u). Easy: intros [H| |] ; discriminate? value's constructors: v_abs : value (tm_abs T t), v_true, v_false. So inversion/discriminate.

OK so this is workable! Let me now define everything concretely and carefully, then write the Rocq code.

Definitions:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
        forall Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body ->
          forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open body u))
  end.
```

Note: `multi step t v` — notation t -->* v.

Lemmas:

1. `R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.`
   destruct T; simpl; intros; [exact H | exact (proj1 H)].

Hmm, `R T t` when T is a variable: destruct T then simpl in H. Since R is a Fixpoint, `simpl` unfolds. Let me write:
```
Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros T t H. destruct T as [| A B].
  - simpl in H. exact H.
  - simpl in H. exact (proj1 H).
Qed.
```
Careful: destructing T with the hypothesis H : R T t; after destruct, H : R Ty_Bool t = match Ty_Bool ... reduces to SN t. `simpl in H` should reduce. Good.

2. `value_no_step : forall v t, value v -> v --> t -> False.`
   Proof: intros v t Hv Hstep. destruct Hv; inversion Hstep.
   For v_abs: Hstep : tm_abs T t0 --> t1. Inversion on step with impossible indices. Should be solved by `inversion Hstep` producing no goals? It might leave a goal if inversion doesn't close. Typically `inversion Hstep` in such a case solves the goal since all constructors are contradictory. If not, use `inversion Hstep; subst; discriminate` etc. Let's see: step constructors:
   ST_AppAbs : forall T t v, lc (abs T t) -> value v -> app (abs T t) v --> open t v.
   Inverting `tm_abs T0 t0 --> t1`: Coq tries to unify tm_abs T0 t0 with tm_app ... — fails, so no cases; inversion should close the goal. I think `inversion Hstep` closes it. Fine, if not, `inversion Hstep` then done via `discriminate`? Let's include `inversion Hstep` and see.

   Actually, careful: `destruct Hv` where Hv : value v. If v is a variable, destruct gives three cases: v = tm_abs T t (with H : locally_closed ...) — hmm, destruct on an inductive with indices: value (tm_abs T t) — the constructors' arguments. destruct should substitute v := tm_abs T t. Fine.

3. `value_sn : forall v, value v -> strongly_normalizing v.`
   Proof: intros v Hv. apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. exfalso. apply (value_no_step v t' Hv Hstep).

4. `multi_trans : forall x y z, x -->* y -> y -->* z -> x -->* z.`
   Proof: intros x y z H. induction H; intros H2; [exact H2 | apply multi_step with y; assumption...]. Actually:
   induction H as [x | x y z Hxy Hyz IH]; intros H2.
   - exact H2.

```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom := ...
Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L).
Lemma le_fold_max : forall L x, In x L -> x <= fold_right Nat.max 0 L.
Proof. induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x Hx.
 - inversion Hx.
 - simpl. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
   + apply Nat.le_max_l.
   + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; exact Hx | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.
Lemma fresh_not_in : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof. intros L H. unfold fresh in H. apply le_fold_max in H. apply Nat.nle_succ_diag_l in ... 
```

Substitution commutation: 
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```

```
Lemma R_app_aux : forall A B t u, SN t -> Hclos ... -> R A u -> R B (t u).

  intros B Hclos.

  revert B Hclos.

  value t ->
  Hclos A B t ->
  SN u ->

       Hclos with v := t (multi_refl), value v := Hval, Tbody := T, body := t0, eq refl, w := u0, HRu gives R Bool (open t0 u0) → SN ✓.

    + value clause: intros v Hm Hv Tbody body Heq w HRw.
      (* v is a value reachable from t u; v = abs Tbody body; need R B2 (open body w) *)
      ...

Hmm, in the arrow case's value clause: we need to prove R B2 (open body w) where v = abs Tbody body is reachable. Analysis of Hm : t u -->* v. Since t u is an app and v is a value, Hm is not refl. Inverting multi step (app t u) v gives two cases:
  - multi_refl: v = app t u, contradiction with value v (app not a value).
  - multi_step: there exist y, z with x := app t u, and step (app t u) y, multi y z, z = v. So the first step is exposed ✓. So `inversion Hm` gives us the first step and the rest.

Actually, `inversion` on an inductive family with two constructors where the index is a specific term: for multi_refl, it unifies `app t u` with `v` giving v = app t u; then Hv : value (app t u) → contradiction. For multi_step, it gives some y, z and equations. We can then destruct the step y via inversion.

Now let's define R_app in terms of R_app_aux:
```
Lemma R_app : forall A B t u, R (Ty_Arrow A B) t -> R A u -> R B (tm_app t u).
Proof.
  intros A B t u HRt HRu.
  destruct (R_sn _ _ HRt) ... 
  (* R (A→B) t unfolds to SN t /\ Hclos *)
  apply R_app_aux with (A:=A) (B:=B).
  - exact (proj1 HRt)   (* after simpl *)
  - simpl in HRt; exact (proj2 HRt)
  - exact HRu
Qed.
```
Careful with simpl: R (Ty_Arrow A B) t is a Fixpoint applied to a constructor, so it reduces to the conjunction. `simpl in HRt` should give HRt : SN t ∧ Hclos. Or `destruct HRt as [HSN Hclos]` after `simpl in HRt`. Since R's arrow branch is `strongly_normalizing t /\ (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> forall Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body -> forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open body u))` — note the recursive calls R T1 u and R T2 (open body u) are inside; simpl may unfold those too if T1/T2 are constructors, but they're variables so it stops. ✓

Now, SN_intro usage. Given HSNt : SN t, I want the "∀ reducts SN" and the induction principle. In Rocq:
```

```
When HSNt : strongly_normalizing t and t is a variable, this should work: t0 is fresh, Hred : ∀ t', t0 --> t' → SN t', IH : ∀ t', t0 --> t' → (motive). But does it substitute t := t0? Since SN_intro : ∀ t, (∀t', t --> t' → SN t') → SN t, and HSNt : SN t, destruct/induction on it gives t0 and an equation? Since t is a variable, Coq can unify t := t0 directly (no equation). Usually `induction H` on SN t works if t is a variable in context (Coq will clear it and reintroduce as t0 or keep the name). Let me be careful: pattern `induction HSNt as [t0 Hred IH]` — the constructor has 2 arguments: t and the function. The induction principle SN_ind: ∀ P, (∀ t0 H, (∀ t', t0 --> t' → P t') → P t0) → ∀ t, SN t → P t. So `induction HSNt as [t0 Hred IH]` gives goal P t0 with IH : ∀t', t0 --> t' → P t'. Since our goal mentions t (the original), and t is a variable, Coq will substitute t := t0 in the goal and revert dependencies. Should be OK.

  strongly_normalizing t ->

Now, the fundamental theorem. Let me write it.

```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...

Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, (forall x A, Gamma x = Some A -> R A (sigma x)) -> R T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H. induction H as [...]; intros sigma Hsigma.

  - pose (sigma' := fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y).

  - Apply IHx with sigma': need ∀ y A, update Gamma x T1 y = Some A → R A (sigma' y).

Hmm, inversion HSN where HSN : SN t. The constructor SN_intro gives an argument Hred : ∀t', t --> t' → SN t'. inversion may create t0 and equation t0 = t; `inversion HSN; subst` hmm. Let me think about `destruct HSN as [Hred]` — the constructor SN_intro has two arguments (t and Hred), but the first is the index. In Coq, `destruct HSN` on `SN_intro t Hred : SN t` yields Hred with the index unified. The pattern `[Hred]`? The number of arguments in the pattern corresponds to the constructor's arguments excluding... hmm, `destruct H as [a b]` for `H : exists x, P x` gives a, b. For `SN t` where SN_intro : ∀t, (...) → SN t, the arguments are t and the function. But `t` is the index (appears in the conclusion), so `destruct HSN as [Hred]` may give just the non-index argument? I think for inductives where the conclusion is `SN t` with t an argument, all constructor arguments are binders in the constructor type: SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t. When destructing `H : SN t`, Coq will generalize and give you the constructor's arguments with t instantiated: so you get one argument: the function. Pattern [Hred] should work? Or maybe you need [t0 Hred]? Common Rocq idiom: `induction H as [t Hred IH]` for SN! I recall from SF: `induction H as [t Hred IH]`. Hmm, in SF's definitions SN is `Inductive sn : tm -> Prop := sn_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> sn t') -> sn t.` and proofs often do `induction Hsn as [t Hred IH]`. That suggests the pattern includes t. But t is the index... In Coq, when destructing/inducting a hypothesis `H : sn t` where t is a variable, if you provide a pattern [t0 Hred IH], it might complain about the number of patterns. Actually, the constructor sn_intro's arguments as displayed by `Check sn_intro`: `forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> sn t') -> sn t`. When you destruct `H : sn t`, Coq abstracts t, so the goal becomes ∀ t, sn t → ... and it destructs sn t into the constructor: the arguments are t and the function. So the pattern [t0 Hred] or [t0 Hred IH] (with IH for the recursive occurrences in the function argument? The function argument contains sn t' occurrences, so the induction principle may generate IHs). For `destruct`, no IHs; for `induction`, Coq generates IH for the recursive occurrences: the argument `forall t', step t t' -> sn t'` is a function type with sn occurrences to the left of arrows — the induction principle sn_ind : ∀P, (∀t, (∀t', step t t' → P t') → P t) → ∀t, sn t → P t. So `induction H as [t0 Hred IH]` gives IH : ∀t', step t0 t' → P t'. Yes! The IH is a function over t'. So the pattern [t0 Hred IH] matches the induction principle: two constructor arguments (t0, Hred) plus the derived IH. ✓

Actually simplest: 
```
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' HSN Hstep. induction HSN as [t0 Hred IH]. apply Hred. exact Hstep.
Qed.
```
Hmm, after induction, goal is SN t' with t0 substituted for t. Should work.

Theorem normalization : forall t T, <{ empty |-- t \in T }> -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  pose proof (fundamental empty t T Hty (fun x => tm_fvar x)) as HR.
  - (* side condition *) intros x A Hx. unfold empty in Hx. discriminate. 
```

```
  assert (Hmatch : forall x A, empty x = Some A -> R A (tm_fvar x)).
  { intros x A Hx. unfold empty in Hx. discriminate. }
  pose proof (fundamental empty t T Hty (fun x => tm_fvar x) Hmatch) as HR.
  rewrite subst_id in HR.  (* or change *)
  apply R_sn in HR. exact HR.
Qed.
```
Where subst_id : ∀ t, subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.

Additional details:

fv function:
```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fv t1) (fv t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.
```

subst:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```

subst_open:
```
Lemma subst_open : forall sigma k u t,
  subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma k u t. revert k. induction t; intros k; simpl.
  - (* bvar *) destruct (Nat.eqb k n); reflexivity.
  - (* fvar *) reflexivity.
  - (* app *) rewrite IHt1, IHt2. reflexivity.
  - (* abs *) simpl. rewrite IHt. reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```
Careful: the induction on t generates IHt1, IHt2 etc. For abs, the goal after simpl: subst sigma (open_rec (S k) u t) = open_rec (S k) (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t), which is IHt (S k)... but IHt : ∀ k, ... with k generalized? Since we reverted k before induction, IHt : ∀ k, subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = ... ✓. But careful with the naming; and the `simpl` may unfold `open_rec` for the abs case: open_rec k u (tm_abs T t) = tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) u t) ✓.

Also for the bvar case: open_rec k u (tm_bvar n) = if Nat.eqb k n then u else tm_bvar n. So subst sigma (...) = if eqb then subst sigma u else tm_bvar n; RHS open_rec k (subst sigma u) (tm_bvar n) = if eqb k n then subst sigma u else tm_bvar n ✓ (need eqb same; destruct).

For app: LHS = tm_app (subst sigma (open_rec k u t1)) (subst sigma (open_rec k u t2)); RHS = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t1) app... ✓ with IH.

subst_agree:
```
Lemma subst_agree : forall t sigma sigma',
  (forall x, In x (fv t) -> sigma x = sigma' x) ->
  subst sigma t = subst sigma' t.
Proof.
  intros t. induction t; intros sigma sigma' Hagree; simpl.
  - reflexivity.
  - apply Hagree. simpl. auto.  (* In x (x::nil) *)
  - rewrite IHt1, IHt2; [reflexivity | | ]; intros x Hx; apply Hagree; simpl; apply in_or_app; ...
```
Simpler: for app, Hagree : ∀ x, In x (fv t1 ++ fv t2) → ...; need for t1: In x (fv t1) → sigma x = sigma' x — follows from in_or_app (left). Use `intros x Hx; apply Hagree; apply in_or_app; left; exact Hx`.
  - abs: rewrite IHt; [reflexivity | intros x Hx; apply Hagree; exact Hx].
  - true/false: reflexivity.

```

fresh:
```
Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L).
Lemma le_fold_max : forall L x, In x L -> x <= fold_right Nat.max 0 L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x Hx.
  - inversion Hx.
  - simpl. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + etransitivity; [apply IH; exact Hx | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.
Lemma fresh_not_in : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hx. unfold fresh in Hx.
  apply le_fold_max in Hx.
  apply (Nat.nle_succ_diag_l (fold_right Nat.max 0 L)). exact Hx.
Qed.
```
Check: Nat.nle_succ_diag_l : forall n : nat, ~ S n <= n. ✓ In Stdlib Arith.PeanoNat.

Hmm, `le_fold_max` with destruct Hx as [-> | Hx]: In x (a :: L) = x = a ∨ In x L. ✓

Now, in the fundamental T_Abs case, we choose x := fresh (L ++ fv t1). Then:
- ¬ In x L: since In x (L ++ fv t1) would follow from In x L via in_or_app left.
- ¬ In x (fv t1): similar.
```
Lemma fresh_not_in_app_l : forall L1 L2, ~ In (fresh (L1 ++ L2)) L1.
Lemma fresh_not_in_app_r : forall L1 L2, ~ In (fresh (L1 ++ L2)) L2.
```
Prove via fresh_not_in and in_app_iff (List.in_app_iff : In a (l ++ l') <-> In a l \/ In a l').

Now the R definition. Let me write it and check Rocq accepts the Fixpoint:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
        forall (Tbody : ty) (body : tm), v = tm_abs Tbody body ->
          forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open body u))
  end.
```
Rocq's guard checker: recursive calls R T1 u and R T2 (open body u) — T1 and T2 are subterms of T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2 ✓. The Fixpoint has principal argument T (first argument). ✓

Now `simpl` on `R (Ty_Arrow A B) t` unfolds ✓. `simpl` on `R Ty_Bool t` gives SN t ✓.

strongly_normalizing_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop,

Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.

  - simpl in H. exact H.
  - simpl in H. exact (proj1 H).
Qed.
```

R_step:
```
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T t t' H Hstep. destruct T as [| A B].
  - simpl in *. apply SN_step with (t := t); assumption.
  - simpl in H. simpl. split.
    + apply SN_step with (t := t); [exact (proj1 H) | exact Hstep].
    + intros v Hm Hval Tbody body Heq w HRw.
      apply (proj2 H v); [| exact Hval | exact Heq | exact HRw].
      apply multi_step with (y := t'); [exact Hstep | exact Hm].
Qed.
```
Careful: `apply (proj2 H v)` — proj2 H : ∀v, multi step t v → value v → ∀Tbody body, v = abs Tbody body → ∀u, R A u → R B (open body u). Applying to v gives the rest ✓.

R_multi:
```
Lemma R_multi : forall T t t', R T t -> t -->* t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T t t' H Hm. induction Hm as [x | x y z Hxy Hyz IH].
  - exact H.
  - apply IH. apply R_step with (t := x); [exact H | exact Hxy].
Qed.
```
Hmm, induction Hm with H in context mentioning t (= x in refl case). Dependency: H : R T t and Hm : t -->* t'. If t is a variable, induction will abstract it and H must be reverted (auto). The refl case: goal R T x with H : R T x ✓. Step case: H : R T x, Hxy : x --> y, Hyz : y -->* z, IH : R T y → R T z. Goal R T z. `apply IH; apply R_step with x` ✓. But will the auto-revert produce the right IH? The motive P x y := R T x → R T y. refl: ∀x, R T x → R T x ✓. step: ∀x y z, step x y → multi y z → (R T y → R T z) → R T x → R T z ✓. So IH : R T y → R T z ✓. So I need to intro H in each case: `intros H` maybe. Let me write:
```
  intros T t t' H Hm. revert H. induction Hm as [x | x y z Hxy Hyz IH]; intros H.
  - exact H.
  - apply IH. exact (R_step T x y H Hxy).
```
Hmm, R_step's argument order: forall T t t', R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'. So `R_step T x y H Hxy` ✓.

Wait, does `revert H` work when H mentions t and the goal mentions t? After revert H, goal: R T t → t -->* t'. Then induction Hm abstracts t, t' ✓.

Lemma multi_trans : forall x y z, x -->* y -> y -->* z -> x -->* z.

multi_ind : forall (X) (R) (P : X -> X -> Prop),
  (forall x, P x x) ->
  (forall x y z, R x y -> multi R y z -> P y z -> P x z) ->
  forall x y, multi R x y -> P x y

multi_sn:
```
Lemma multi_sn : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> t -->* t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' HSN Hm. revert HSN. induction Hm as [x | x y z Hxy Hyz IH]; intros HSN.

Qed.

multi_stuck (for abs):
```
Lemma multi_stuck : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> False) -> forall t', t -->* t' -> t' = t.
Proof.
  intros t Hns t' Hm. revert Hns. induction Hm as [x | x y z Hxy Hyz IH]; intros Hns.
  - reflexivity.
  - exfalso. exact (Hns y Hxy).
Qed.
```
Check: after revert Hns, goal `(∀t', t --> t' → False) → t' = t`; induction Hm abstracts t and t'. refl case: x; goal (∀t', x --> t' → False) → x = x ✓. step case: x y z, Hxy : x --> y, Hyz, IH : (∀t', y --> t' → False) → z = y; goal (∀t', x --> t' → False) → z = x; intro Hns; exfalso; exact (Hns y Hxy) ✓.

Now R_abs:
```
Lemma R_abs : forall A B Tbody body,
  (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u)) ->
  R (Ty_Arrow A B) (tm_abs Tbody body).
Proof.
  intros A B Tbody body Hbody. simpl. split.
  - apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. exfalso. exact (abs_no_step Tbody body t' Hstep).
  - intros v Hm Hval Tbody' body' Heq w HRw.
    assert (Hv : v = tm_abs Tbody body).
    { symmetry. apply (multi_stuck (tm_abs Tbody body)); [intros t' Hs; exact (abs_no_step Tbody body t' Hs) | exact Hm]. }
    subst v. injection Heq as HT Hb. subst Tbody' body'.
    apply Hbody. exact HRw.
Qed.
```
Hmm, `subst v` where Hv : v = tm_abs Tbody body ✓. Then Heq : tm_abs Tbody body = tm_abs Tbody' body'. injection Heq as HT Hb — injection on ty equality gives Tbody = Tbody' and body = body'; names. `subst Tbody' body'`: with HT : Tbody = Tbody' and Hb : body = body', subst should replace. Then goal R B (open body w) and Hbody : ∀u, R A u → R B (open body u). apply Hbody; exact HRw ✓.

Actually, simplification: after subst v, the goal is R B (open body' w). We can rewrite ← Hb. Let me use subst carefully.

Wait, there's an issue: multi_stuck's statement has t as a parameter and returns t' = t. With t := tm_abs Tbody body, Hm : tm_abs Tbody body -->* v, we get v = tm_abs Tbody body ✓.

Now R_app_value and R_app_aux. Let me write them carefully in code, then iterate.

R_app_value:
```
Lemma R_app_value : forall A B (t u : tm),
  value t ->
  (forall v, t -->* v -> value v ->
     forall (Tbody : ty) (body : tm), v = tm_abs Tbody body ->
       forall w, R A w -> R B (open body w)) ->
  strongly_normalizing u ->
  R A u ->
  R B (tm_app t u).
Proof.
  intros A B t u Hval Hclos HSNu HRu.
  revert B Hclos HRu.
  induction HSNu as [u0 Hured IHu].
  intros B Hclos HRu.
  destruct B as [| B1 B2].
  - simpl. apply SN_intro. intros w Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.
    + (* AppAbs *) 
      ... Hclos ...
    + (* App1 *) exfalso; exact (value_no_step t t1' Hval H).
    + (* App2 *) apply R_sn with (T := Ty_Bool). apply IHu; [exact H | exact Hclos | apply R_step with (t := u0); assumption ].
  - simpl. split.
    + apply SN_intro. intros w Hstep. inversion Hstep; subst.
      * ...
      * ...
      * ...
    + intros v Hm Hvalv Tbody body Heq w0 HRw.
      inversion Hm as [Heq0 | y z Hstep Hyz Heqz]; subst.

Lemma R_app_aux : forall A B (t u : tm),

Code:

```coq
(** Free variables and substitution on term variables. *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fv t1) (fv t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right Nat.max 0 L).

Lemma le_fold_max : forall (L : list atom) (x : atom),
  In x L -> x <= fold_right Nat.max 0 L.
Proof.
  intros L. induction L as [| a L IH]; intros x Hx.
  - inversion Hx.
  - simpl in Hx. simpl. destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; exact Hx | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_not_in : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hx. unfold fresh in Hx. apply le_fold_max in Hx.
  apply (Nat.nle_succ_diag_l (fold_right Nat.max 0 L)). exact Hx.
Qed.
```

Lemma subst_open : forall sigma k u t,
  subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma k u t. revert k.
  induction t as [n | x | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | | ]; intros k; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k n); reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2. reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH. reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Substitution of lc terms (σ images lc) preserves lc. So we need σ images lc again — but only for fvars actually in t1. Let me step back and consider a cleaner design: use a logical relation that only talks about locally closed terms and contexts with lc images. The fundamental theorem:

For all Γ t T, has_type Γ t T → for all σ, (∀ x A, Γ x = Some A → R A (σ x)) → (∀ x, lc_at 0 (σ x)) → R T (subst σ t).

Hmm, but in the T_Abs case we take σ' = σ[x := u] with u locally closed from R A u (if R implies lc). If R doesn't imply lc we're stuck.

Alternatively, require σ to map ALL atoms to locally closed terms? Then σ' = σ[x := u] works only if u lc. But u is arbitrary with R A u.

So we truly need R A u → lc_at 0 u. Adding lc to R seems necessary. Let's think about whether lc_at 0 (open body u) etc. all work out.

Let me reconsider: maybe a totally different approach avoids substitution into open terms altogether: define the logical relation on *locally closed* terms and prove the fundamental theorem by induction on typing where the context interpretation uses *environments of values*? Hmm.

Actually, here's another thought: the standard locally nameless approach in Charguéraud's "The Locally Nameless Representation" handles this by defining the semantic interpretation of contexts as: each variable x in Γ is interpreted by a *locally closed* term (a "value" in the semantic sense), and substitutions map fvars to locally closed terms. The fundamental theorem then has the hypothesis that σ maps every fvar in Γ to a locally closed term satisfying R. And the R relation itself is often defined only on locally closed terms.

And R_T(t) → lc_at 0 t is usually part of the relation.

Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=

Now, the substitution lemma issue: we need to prove
  subst σ' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst σ t1) u

Let me prove this directly rather than via two lemmas. General statement:

Lemma subst_open_fvar : forall sigma x u t,
  lc_at 0 u ->
  ~ In x (fv t) ->
  subst (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y) (open t (tm_fvar x))
    = open (subst sigma t) u.

Hmm, but there's a subtlety with binder depths: `open t (fvar x)` replaces bvar 0 at depth 0 with fvar x. `open (subst sigma t) u` replaces bvar 0 at depth 0 with u. Consider t = abs T body where body has bvar 1 referring to the outer binder: open t (fvar x) = abs (open_rec 1 (fvar x) body) — replaces bvar 1 at depth 1 with fvar x. Then subst σ' replaces fvar x with u → abs (open_rec 1 u (subst σ body))? And open (subst σ t) u = open (abs (subst σ body)) u = abs (open_rec 1 u (subst σ body)). So we need the generalized statement for open_rec at arbitrary depth k:

Lemma subst_open_rec_fvar : forall sigma x u t k,
  lc_at 0 u ->
  ~ In x (fv t) ->
  subst (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t)
    = open_rec k u (subst sigma t).

Proof by induction on t, generalizing k:
- bvar i: open_rec k (fvar x) (bvar i) = if eqb k i then fvar x else bvar i. LHS = if eqb k i then subst σ' (fvar x) = u else bvar i. Since u lc doesn't matter here; but wait, if the bvar is kept as bvar i, fine. RHS = open_rec k u (subst σ (bvar i)) = open_rec k u (bvar i) = if eqb k i then u else bvar i ✓. Great — this works because open_rec on a bvar is a simple conditional. The lc of u is needed only for fvar replacement under binders: if σ' replaces fvar x with u, and u contains bvar 0, and this happens under a binder... Let's see where lc is used. In the fvar case y ≠ x: both sides = σ y ✓ (no opening applied). In the fvar case y = x: both sides = u ✓ — hmm, no opening applied either! The RHS open_rec k u (subst σ (fvar x)) = open_rec k u (σ x), and LHS = subst σ' (open_rec k (fvar x) (fvar x)) = subst σ' (fvar x) = u. These are equal only if open_rec k u (σ x) = u. With σ x possibly containing bvars and being opened! Hmm: LHS = u, RHS = open_rec k u (σ x). These are NOT equal in general.

Wait, I need to be careful: the RHS is open_rec k u (subst sigma t) — sigma here is the ORIGINAL sigma, so subst sigma (fvar x) = sigma x, which could be anything. Hmm, but in our usage, do we have x ∉ fv t, so t = fvar x would violate x ∉ fv t! Indeed! In the lemma we have ~ In x (fv t), so the case t = fvar x is excluded. Good. For t = fvar y with y ≠ x: LHS = subst σ' (fvar y) = σ' y = σ y (since y ≠ x); RHS = open_rec k u (σ y) — hmm, this applies open_rec to σ y! If σ y is a bvar, RHS ≠ σ y. Not equal in general!

Hmm. So the lemma is false in general, unless σ y is locally closed for all y. Right — this is the same issue: substitution images need to be locally closed for opening to commute.

But wait: in our fundamental theorem, σ' maps y to σ y for y ≠ x; σ y is locally closed by the hypothesis "all σ images lc". So if we add the hypothesis ∀y, lc_at 0 (σ y), then open_rec k u (σ y) = σ y ✓ (opening a locally closed term does nothing? Careful: open_rec k u (σ y) replaces bvar (k+d) with u; since σ y is locally closed, no bvars at all ✓). So we need a lemma: lc_at 0 t → open_rec k u t = t. ✓ Easy induction.

So the corrected lemma:
Lemma subst_open_fvar : forall sigma x u t k,
  lc_at 0 u ->
  (forall y, lc_at 0 (sigma y)) ->
  ~ In x (fv t) ->
  subst (fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t)
    = open_rec k u (subst sigma t).

Wait, but the LHS substitution is σ' and its images: σ' x = u (lc), σ' y = σ y (lc) ✓.

Definitions:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => lc_at 0 t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      lc_at 0 t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->

Let me redo R_step with lc:
```
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T t t' H Hstep. destruct T as [| A B].
  - simpl in H. simpl. split.
    + apply (step_lc t t' Hstep (proj1 H)).
    + apply SN_step with (t := t); [exact (proj2 H) | exact Hstep].
  - simpl in H. simpl. destruct H as [Hlc [Hsn Hclos]]. split; [| split].
    + apply (step_lc t t' Hstep Hlc).
    + apply SN_step with (t := t); assumption.
    + intros v Hm Hval Tbody body Heq w HRw.
      apply (Hclos v); [| exact Hval | exact Heq | exact HRw].
      apply multi_step with (y := t'); [exact Hstep | exact Hm].
Qed.
```

R_multi similar.

R_abs:
```
Lemma R_abs : forall A B Tbody body,
  lc_at 1 body ->
  (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u)) ->
  R (Ty_Arrow A B) (tm_abs Tbody body).
Proof.
  intros A B Tbody body Hlc Hbody. simpl. split; [| split].
  - exact Hlc.  (* lc_at 0 (abs Tbody body) = lc_at 1 body, definitional? lc_at 0 (tm_abs Tbody body) reduces to... lc_at is inductive; lc_at 0 (tm_abs Tbody body) is a proposition, not definitionally equal to lc_at 1 body. Hmm! lc_at 0 (tm_abs Tbody body) is proved by the constructor lc_abs : lc_at k (tm_abs T t1) when lc_at (S k) t1. So to prove lc_at 0 (abs Tbody body) from lc_at 1 body: apply lc_abs with (k := 0) ✓ or `constructor; exact Hlc`. *)
  - apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. exfalso. exact (abs_no_step Tbody body t' Hstep).
  - intros v Hm Hval Tbody' body' Heq w HRw.
    assert (Hv : v = tm_abs Tbody body) by (symmetry; apply (multi_stuck (tm_abs Tbody body)); [intros t' Hs; exact (abs_no_step Tbody body t' Hs) | exact Hm]).
    subst v. injection Heq as HT Hb. subst Tbody' body'.
    apply Hbody. exact HRw.
Qed.
```

Hmm, `subst v` after Hv : v = tm_abs Tbody body: `subst v` should work if v is a variable. But wait, Hval mentions v; subst replaces it. ✓.

Actually, note `symmetry; apply multi_stuck...`: multi_stuck gives `v = tm_abs Tbody body`? Its statement: forall t, (∀t', t --> t' → False) → ∀t', t -->* t' → t' = t. So with t = abs, t' = v, gives v = abs ✓. So no symmetry needed.

Now, R_app_value with lc:
```
Lemma R_app_value : forall A B (t u : tm),
  value t ->
  (forall v, t -->* v -> value v ->
     forall Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body ->
       forall w, R A w -> R B (open body w)) ->
  strongly_normalizing u ->
  R A u ->

Let me rewrite fv_open_rec properly:

```
Lemma fv_open_rec : forall (t : tm) (x : atom) (k : nat) (y : atom),
    In y (fv t) -> y = x \/ In y (fv (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t)).
Proof.
  induction t as [i | a | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | | ];
    intros x k y H; simpl in *.
  - inversion H.
  - right. exact H.
  - apply in_app_iff in H. destruct H as [H | H].
    + destruct (IH1 x k y H) as [-> | H']; [left; reflexivity |].
      right. apply in_or_app. left. exact H'.
    + destruct (IH2 x k y H) as [-> | H']; [left; reflexivity |].
      right. apply in_or_app. right. exact H'.
  - right. apply IH with (x := x) (k := k). exact H.

Let me write it:
```
Lemma lc_at_le : forall (k k' : nat) (t : tm), k <= k' -> lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t.
Proof.
  intros k k' t Hle H. revert k' Hle.
  induction H as [k0 i Hlt | k0 x | k0 a b Ha IHa Hb IHb | k0 T body Hbody IH | k0 | k0];
    intros k' Hle.
  - apply lc_bvar. eapply Nat.lt_le_trans; [exact Hlt | exact Hle].
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; [apply IHa; exact Hle | apply IHb; exact Hle].
  - apply lc_abs. apply IH. apply Nat.succ_le_mono. exact Hle.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.
```
Then in lc_subst's fvar case: `apply (lc_at_le 0 k (sigma x)); [apply Nat.le_0_l | apply Hsigma]`.

Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.

R_step:
```
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T t t' H Hstep. destruct T as [| A B]; simpl in *.
  - split.
    + apply (step_lc t t' Hstep (proj1 H)).
    + apply SN_step with (t := t); [exact (proj2 H) | exact Hstep].
  - destruct H as [Hlc [Hsn Hclos]]. split; [| split].
    + apply (step_lc t t' Hstep Hlc).
    + apply SN_step with (t := t); assumption.
    + intros v Hm Hval Tbody body Heq w HRw.
      apply (Hclos v); [| exact Hval | exact Heq | exact HRw].
      apply multi_step with (y := t'); [exact Hstep | exact Hm].
Qed.
```

R_multi:
```
Lemma R_multi : forall T t t', R T t -> t -->* t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  intros T t t' H Hm. revert H.
  induction Hm as [x | x y z Hxy Hyz IH]; intros H.
  - exact H.
  - apply IH. apply (R_step T x y H Hxy).
Qed.
```

R_abs:
```
Lemma R_abs : forall (A B : ty) (Tbody : ty) (body : tm),
  lc_at 1 body ->
  (forall u, R A u -> R B (open body u)) ->
  R (Ty_Arrow A B) (tm_abs Tbody body).
Proof.
  intros A B Tbody body Hlc Hbody. simpl. split; [| split].
  - apply lc_abs. exact Hlc.
  - apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep. exfalso. exact (abs_no_step Tbody body t' Hstep).
  - intros v Hm Hval Tbody' body' Heq w HRw.
    assert (Hv : v = tm_abs Tbody body).
    { apply (multi_stuck (tm_abs Tbody body)).
      - intros t' Hs. exact (abs_no_step Tbody body t' Hs).
      - exact Hm. }
    subst v. injection Heq as HT Hb. subst Tbody' body'.
    apply Hbody. exact HRw.
Qed.
```

Definition Vclause (B : ty) (w : tm) : Prop :=
  match B with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow B1 B2 =>
      forall v, w -->* v -> value v -> forall Tbody body, v = tm_abs Tbody body ->
        forall u, R B1 u -> R B2 (open body u)
  end.

But Vclause can't be a Fixpoint calling R before R is defined... we can define it after R as a Definition (it's a match on B producing a Prop using R, which is already defined) ✓. Then:

Let me do that.

Define:
```
Definition Vclause (B : ty) (w : tm) : Prop :=
  match B with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow B1 B2 =>
      forall v, multi step w v -> value v ->
        forall (Tbody : ty) (body : tm), v = tm_abs Tbody body ->
          forall u, R B1 u -> R B2 (open body u)
  end.
```
Then:
```
Lemma R_iff : forall B w, R B w <-> lc_at 0 w /\ strongly_normalizing w /\ Vclause B w.
Proof.
  intros B w. destruct B as [| B1 B2]; simpl.
  - split.
    + intros [H1 H2]. split; [exact H1 | split; [exact H2 | exact I]].
    + intros [H1 [H2 _]]. split; assumption.
  - split; intros H; destruct H as [H1 [H2 H3]]; split; [exact H1 | split; [exact H2 | exact H3]].
    (* and back *)
Qed.
```
Hmm, for arrow, R is definitionally lc ∧ SN ∧ Vclause, so `tauto`-ish. Let me write it directly.

Then when proving R B (app t u) uniformly, I can:
apply R_iff; split; [lc | split; [SN | value clause]].

And in the value clause, destruct B to reveal it's Vclause.

This makes the proofs uniform! Great. Let me use this.
